    The search didn't turn up the Kaggle dataset or anyone named Vadimkin, so I can't confirm it exists or what it contains. The results cover related Ukrainian air-alarm data sources:
    
    - **Alarm app data:** El País's visual team used alarm data collated by Ukrainian developer Vadym Klymenko, drawn from alerts sent by the єТривога app based on users' locations.
    - **Alerts.in.ua:** A volunteer site that has been collecting data since the first day of Russia's full-scale invasion. Its 2022 total was about 14,870 alarms, per the volunteer site alerts.in.ua, which also noted that the number and duration of air alerts are approximate, especially early in the invasion.
    - **Official 2022 count:** The State Border Guard Service reported the same 14,870 figure for 2022 (Ukrainian State Border Guard Service, an Ukrinform correspondent reports).
    - **2023 count:** A Kyiv Post analysis reported a total of 6,807 alerts thus far in 2023 as of its writing.
    
    For the Kaggle dataset itself, I'd suggest searching Kaggle directly for the uploader's username or for "Ukraine air alarms," since the web search didn't index it. Check its license and the source of its alarm data before relying on it.

    The search turned up mostly background on the service and its client libraries, with little on the API's own version history.
    
    - **Service origins:** One source says alerts.in.ua is an online service that visualizes information about air alerts and other threats on the map of Ukraine. Its idea appeared in the first weeks of the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine, and a Telegram channel followed in March 2022 (Ajax Systems together with Stfalcon and with support from the Ministry of Digital Transformation announced the official Telegram channel "Air Raid Alert.").
    - **API timeline:** The clearest date is from a 2026 article, which says since 2022, an official API has enabled volunteer maps to receive near real-time data. That source doesn't give a launch date or version history for the API itself.
    - **Data sourcing:** For its 2022 alarm statistics, the alerts.in.ua team used data from local mass media, "Suspilne", channels of heads of regional administrations, mobile applications, and other public sources. The same source notes that the number and duration of air alerts are approximate for early 2022.
    - **Client libraries:** The official Python client is documented on GitHub and PyPI. It has sync and async clients, and you need an API token by submitting an API request form.
    - **Caveat:** One result was an api.alerts.in.ua page styled as a military situation report dated September 2026. It describes itself as not an official military document, so I wouldn't treat it as authoritative. A separate third-party project (raid on GitHub) uses alerts.com.ua, not alerts.in.ua, so it isn't the same service.
    
    For the API's version history, check the official API documentation or changelog on alerts.in.ua directly; I didn't find that in these results.

    The search didn't turn up a GitHub repo of historical air raid alert CSVs. The results are mostly live tools and unrelated conflict datasets.
    
    - **Live alert API:** alerts.in.ua publishes a current status feed in markdown, not a historical archive. It's open-source civil-defence alert monitoring, so it's useful for live data, not history.
    - **Python module:** The AirAlerts package on PyPI is a module for monitoring Air Raid Alerts in Ukraine. It returns real-time status by oblast, not a historical log.
    - **Possible GitHub project:** A conference slide deck links to https://github.com/sortafreel/dmbm, which covers how alerts are collected and visualized. I couldn't confirm from the results whether it contains a historical CSV, so check the repo directly.
    - **Conflict datasets (not alert data):** The UMD conflict tracking dataset on bsos-data.umd.edu is a monthly CSV of events, fatalities, and aid. It doesn't include air raid alerts. The Economist's fire data on Kaggle is updated from the Economist's Ukraine war-fire GitHub repository, and it tracks satellite-detected fires, not sirens.
    
    A follow-up search for terms like "alarm map historical data" or "air alert archive csv github" would be the next step to find an actual historical alert dataset.

    ## 🚨 About Datasets
    
    There are two sources of alerts: official
    and unofficial (collected by volunteers from [eTryvoga](https://app.etryvoga.com) channel).
    
    Both datasets will be updated daily. All times are in UTC.
    
    ### Official dataset
    
    You may see `official_data_en.csv` (🇬🇧) and `official_data_uk.csv` (🇺🇦) files.
    They're identical but in different language.
    
    Official dataset contains information from 15th of March 2022 – it's the first day when siren record occurs.
    Since December, 2025th there is mostly air raid alerts on raion (district) level ([see post in the media](https://texty.org.ua/fragments/116501/v-ukrayini-zaprovadzhuyetsya-novyj-pidxid-do-oholoshennya-povitryanyx-tryvoh-svyrydenko/)), before sirens were applied to the whole oblast (region). You may find them on [wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raions_of_Ukraine).
    On some .geojson files they're Subnational Administrative Boundaries (Admin 2: 139 Raion (District))
    
    ### Volunteer datasets
    
    Data by volunteers are stored in `volunteer_data_uk.csv` (🇺🇦) and `volunteer_data_en.csv` (🇬🇧).
    
    It contains more data (starts from 25th of February – second day of war!) and only on oblast (region) level.
    
    If there are no messages about the end of the sirens,
    you may see them with `naive=True` and `finished_at = started_at + 30 minutes`.
    
    Thanks to [eTryvoga](https://app.etryvoga.com) channel for this data.
    
    ## 🤔 Good to Know
    
    There are two permanent sirens:
    
    1. In **Luhansk region** from April 4 at 04:45 PM (UTC+00) or April 4 at 07:45 PM (local time)
    2. In **Crimea** from December 10 at 10:22 PM (UTC+00) or December 11 at 12:22 AM (local time)
    
    They are not listed in datasets, so you may want to process them manually.

==== CLAUDE (2026-10-12 10:20:12 UTC) ====
I have enough to write CLAUDE.md and propose ideas. I'll save the profiling script into an `exploration/` folder so it can be reproduced.
[tool call: Write]
    /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/exploration/01_data_profile.py
    """Download the Vadimkin air-raid sirens dataset and print a coverage profile.
    
    Usage: uv run --with pandas python exploration/01_data_profile.py
    """
    from pathlib import Path
    from urllib.request import urlretrieve
    
    import pandas as pd
    
    BASE = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/main/datasets"
    RAW = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "data" / "raw"
    
    
    def fetch(name: str) -> Path:
        RAW.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        path = RAW / name
        if not path.exists():
            urlretrieve(f"{BASE}/{name}", path)
        return path
    
    
    def main() -> None:
        o = pd.read_csv(fetch("official_data_en.csv"), parse_dates=["started_at", "finished_at"])
        v = pd.read_csv(fetch("volunteer_data_en.csv"), parse_dates=["started_at", "finished_at"])
    
        print("official :", o.started_at.min(), "->", o.started_at.max(), f"({len(o)} rows)")
        print("volunteer:", v.started_at.min(), "->", v.started_at.max(), f"({len(v)} rows)")
        print("volunteer naive share:", round(v.naive.mean(), 3))
    
        # Oblast -> raion regime shift (2025)
        q = o.started_at.dt.tz_localize(None).dt.to_period("Q")
        print(pd.crosstab(q, o.level).tail(8))
    
        dur = (o.finished_at - o.started_at).dt.total_seconds() / 60
        print("official median duration, min:", round(dur.median(), 1))
    
    
    if __name__ == "__main__":
        main()

    # CLAUDE.md
    
    This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.
    
    ## Project
    
    Application pet-project for an AI agentic school: **time series analysis/forecasting of air raid alerts in Ukraine**, in Python.
    - Deadline: **Mon 12 Oct 2026, 23:59 Kyiv time (EEST, UTC+3)**.
    - Deliverables: GitHub repo with working code, full AI conversation log, ~100-word reflection (what went wrong / how the approach changed / why the final version is better).
    - Reviewers grade the *process* (the AI log) as much as the result: iterate, verify claims against data, correct errors explicitly.
    
    ## Hard rules
    
    - **No data leakage.** Every feature used for a forecast made at time `t` must be computable from data with timestamp `< t` (alert *start* times, and *end* times only for alerts already finished by `t`; text only if *published* before `t`). Use time-ordered splits, never random splits.
    - **Evaluate on the most recent period** available (the last weeks of data), against naive baselines (seasonal-naive / historical rate).
    
    ## Data sources (verified 2026-10-12)
    
    Primary: [Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset), raw CSVs under `datasets/`. All times are UTC.
    - `official_data_en.csv`: columns `oblast, raion, hromada, level, started_at, finished_at, source`. Starts 2022-03-15. **Last record is 2026-09-07 05:28 UTC.** The repo still gets a commit every day, but this file hasn't grown since then. The official Telegram channel `t.me/s/air_alert_ua`, which feeds it, also stops posting at 2026-09-07 06:09 UTC.
    - `volunteer_data_en.csv` (eTryvoga): columns `region, started_at, finished_at, naive`. Oblast level only, 25 regions, starts 2022-02-25, **current to the present day**. `naive=True` (~5%) means the end time is unknown and was set to start + 30 min.
    - **Structural break:** official alerts moved from oblast level to **raion level** (118 raions) during 2025 (raion share rises from Q2 2025; oblast-level is near zero from Q1 2026). Counts per oblast are not comparable across this break unless raion alerts are merged into oblast-level "any alert active" intervals.
    - Permanent alerts (Luhansk since 2022-04-04, Crimea since 2022-12-10) are **not** in the data.
    
    Secondary:
    - `t.me/s/kpszsu` (Ukrainian Air Force, live): public web preview of threat posts (drones, missiles, directions). Scrapeable without auth; each post has a `datetime` that can be used for leakage-safe text features.
    - alerts.in.ua API: requires a token (HTTP 401 without one).
    
    ## Layout & commands
    
    Python 3.12, dependencies run via `uv`.
    - `exploration/`: throwaway profiling scripts. Run: `uv run --with pandas python exploration/01_data_profile.py` (downloads CSVs to `data/raw/`).
    - `data/` is for downloaded/derived data. Don't commit large raw CSVs.

I created `CLAUDE.md`, `exploration/01_data_profile.py` and a `.gitignore` that excludes `data/`. Nothing is committed yet.

**Your deadline is today.** It's 12 October, so there are about 10 hours left until 23:59 Kyiv time. The ideas below are sized for that.

## Data sources (checked against the actual data)

| Source | Coverage | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| [Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset), **official** CSV | 2022‑03‑15 → **2026‑09‑07** | Alerts are at hromada, raion or oblast level. The data ends abruptly, even though the repo gets a commit every day. |
| Same repo, **volunteer** CSV (from the eTryvoga app) | 2022‑02‑25 → **today (12 Oct 04:49 UTC)** | Oblast level only, 25 regions. About 5% of rows are marked `naive`, meaning the end time is a guess (start + 30 min). |
| `t.me/s/kpszsu` (Air Force Telegram channel) | live, readable without login | Threat messages ("drones heading toward X", missile launches) with timestamps. Good optional text input. |
| alerts.in.ua API | — | Returns 401 without a token, and getting one means emailing them. Not realistic for today. |

**Problems I found that change the plan:**
1. **The official data stops on 2026‑09‑07.** The official Telegram channel it comes from also stops at 06:09 UTC that day, partway through a run of alerts. The task says to evaluate on the latest period, so the official data alone can't do that. The volunteer data is the only source that reaches today.
2. **Alerts switched from oblast to district level in 2025.** In Q4 2024 nearly all official alerts were oblast-wide. From late 2025 almost all are per district (raion), and there are 118 districts. A model that counts official alerts per oblast would see a fake jump. The volunteer data stays at oblast level, so it doesn't have this break.
3. The permanent alerts in Luhansk and Crimea aren't in the data, so those regions have to be left out.

## Project ideas

**A. Next-hours alert probability per oblast (my recommendation).** For each oblast and each hour, predict the chance of an alert in the next 1, 3 or 6 hours.
- Train on volunteer data up to about 2026‑09‑12 and test on the last 4 weeks, ending today.
- Inputs: hour of day, day of week, recent alert history in this oblast and its neighbours, and how many oblasts are on alert right now. Countrywide waves of alerts are the strongest signal.
- Model: compare simple baselines ("same hour last week" and the historical rate) with logistic regression and LightGBM. Measure with Brier score, ROC‑AUC and a calibration plot.
- Why I recommend it: it's clearly leakage-safe, small enough to finish today, and the results are easy to explain.

**B. Idea A plus Air Force Telegram messages.** Add counts from `kpszsu` posts as inputs, using only posts published before the forecast time: drone or missile mentions and which oblasts are named. Then measure whether they improve the score. The task lists this as optional, but it's the most interesting add-on. The risk is that scraping the channel and turning its Ukrainian text into features eats hours.

**C. Daily alert-hours forecast.** Predict total alert-hours per oblast for the next 1 to 7 days, using classical time-series models (ARIMA/Prophet-style baselines vs a gradient-boosted model). It's simpler, but less useful in practice and less likely to stand out.

**D. "When will this alert end?"** Given an alert that's in progress, predict how long it will last, using survival analysis. It's novel and useful, but the `naive` end times in the volunteer data add noise to the labels.

**E. Changepoint and spread analysis.** Detect regime shifts and show how a wave of alerts moves from one oblast to its neighbours. Good for visuals, but weak on the required forecasting and evaluation.

My suggestion is **A as the core, then add B only if time allows** (one scraping script and an extra comparison).

Which idea do you want? And do you want to check the September 7 gap yourself first? A screenshot of the official channel stopping would look good in the log.

==== CLAUDE (2026-10-12 12:00:42 UTC) ====
Here's what I found on both questions, plus a few questions back. Nothing is implemented yet.

## 1. Can we get post counts from Telegram for a given day or period?

**Yes, and it's easier than I expected.** Telegram's public web preview (`t.me/s/<channel>`) needs no login or API key. It returns 20 posts per page, and each post has an ID and an exact publish time. I tested it on the Air Force channel `kpszsu`:
- A page loads in about 0.3 s.
- You can **jump straight to any point in history** by post ID, so there's no need to scroll through everything. ID 5000 is from September 2023, 40000 from August 2025, 80000 from 24 September 2026.
- So counting posts per day or per hour is just "fetch pages and group by timestamp".

**How much there is to download:** the channel posts a lot, and more so recently. It averaged about 120 posts/day from April to September 2026 and about 300/day over the last 18 days. All of it is ~85k posts, about 4,300 page requests: roughly 20–30 minutes if Telegram doesn't slow us down, which it might. **The last ~12 months is about half that.** It's a one-off download, and we'd cache it.

**Two caveats:**
- **Edited posts:** the preview shows each post as it reads *now*. If the Air Force later edits a post, we'd see the edited text with the original timestamp. That's a small leakage risk, and the post time and post count aren't affected by it.
- **Raw post counts are a weak signal**, because the channel posts more and more over time. A count of 100 means something different in 2024 than in 2026. Counts relative to the recent average, or *what* the posts mention, are more useful.

## 2. Can your PC run a small embedding model?

Your machine: **Intel i5‑1135G7 (4 cores / 8 threads), 7.6 GB RAM with only ~1.6 GB free right now, integrated Iris Xe graphics (no NVIDIA, so no CUDA).**

- A small multilingual model that handles Ukrainian, like `multilingual-e5-small` (~120M parameters, ~0.5 GB in memory), would run on the CPU at roughly **50–150 short posts per second**. That's an estimate I haven't benchmarked. ~50k posts would take **about 10–20 minutes**, once. Feasible, but **RAM is the bottleneck**: close the browser while it runs.
- **I'd push back on embeddings here, though.** Air Force posts follow a template ("Група ударних БпЛА на Сумщині, курс на …", meaning "Group of attack drones over Sumy region, heading toward …"). Simple rules can pull out **threat type** (drone, ballistic missile, cruise missile, aviation) and **which oblast is mentioned**, and those are exactly the features each oblast's model needs. That's faster, has no RAM concerns, and is easy to explain in an interview. A 384-dimensional embedding vector isn't tied to any oblast and would need squashing down before the model could use it.
- If you want an AI angle on the text, a middle option: **keyword features as the main input, with embeddings as an extra test** ("did embeddings add anything over keywords?"). Either outcome is a good thing to discuss.

Your other plans fit fine. Both models (logistic regression or LightGBM, and a survival model for duration) train on the CPU in seconds to minutes, and the training code and interface will go in separate folders.

## My questions

1. **Map detail: oblast or district (raion)?** The volunteer data goes up to today but is oblast-only, 24 usable oblasts. District-level data exists only in the official file, which stops on 7 September, so we couldn't test on the latest period. **I recommend oblasts.**

2. **What does "starts soon" mean?** Pick the forecast window: e.g. *"P(an alert starts in this oblast within the next 1 hour)"*. 30 min, 1 h and 3 h are the realistic options. We could train several, but one keeps the scope sane.

3. **What should the duration model output?** Options: an expected duration ("~45 min"), probabilities like "P(still active after 30/60/120 min)", or a full survival curve. One detail: alerts in the volunteer data whose end time is a guess have to be dropped or treated as "end unknown", so they don't distort the durations.

4. **Live or replay interface?** By "visualization of the process", do you mean:
   - **(a) Replay:** a time slider over the test period, showing the map of predicted probabilities next to what actually happened, plus scores. Fully reproducible, and the reviewers can run it.
   - **(b) Live:** fetch fresh data now and predict for the current moment.
   - **(c) Both.**

   And what tech for the interface: a Python app (Streamlit/Dash) or a static HTML page the training pipeline exports?

5. **Which Telegram sources?** Just `kpszsu` (official Air Force, structured posts), or also unofficial monitoring channels? Those are faster and more detailed, but noisier and not always reliable.

When you've answered, tell me when to start planning and add your visual-style details.

==== CLAUDE (2026-10-12 12:14:51 UTC) ====
FastAPI is a reasonable choice here, as long as we keep it small. Here's the case for it, the costs, and the alternatives.

## Why FastAPI fits

**The "Now" button needs a server.** Replay alone could be a static HTML file with the predictions baked in, no server at all. But "Now" has to re-download the CSV and run the model in Python, and a static page can't do that. So we need *some* Python backend, and FastAPI is about the smallest one that serves both an API and a web page.

**What it would add to the repo:**
```
app/
  server.py        ~80 lines: loads the trained models, 3 endpoints
  static/
    index.html     the dashboard (layout + dark-theme CSS)
    app.js         map + charts, ~300 lines
    ukraine.geojson
```
The three endpoints:
- `GET /api/replay?t=...` returns predictions vs what actually happened at time t.
- `GET /api/now` re-downloads the data and predicts for the current hour.
- `GET /api/stats` returns the test-period scores.

FastAPI also serves the page itself, so there's no separate frontend build: **no npm, no Node, no React**. The JS libraries load from a CDN with a `<script>` tag.

**What the judges run:**
```
uv sync
uv run python -m training.train     # optional; trained models can be committed (they're small)
uv run python -m app                # then open http://localhost:8000
```
That's one more command than Streamlit, about the same effort.

## Costs

1. **Two languages.** The dashboard is written in JS, not Python. I'd write it; you'd need to read about 300 lines of plain JS to explain it in the interview.
2. **The page needs internet for the CDN libraries.** The judges will have internet. If you want it fully offline, we copy the two library files into the repo.
3. **Port conflicts.** If 8000 is taken, we'd make the port a command-line flag.

## Alternatives

| | Judges run | Palantir look | Python only | "Now" button |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **FastAPI + HTML/JS** | 1 command | ✅ full control | ❌ some JS | ✅ |
| **Streamlit** | 1 command | ⚠️ looks like Streamlit; dense dark panels fight the framework | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Dash (Plotly)** | 1 command | 🟡 decent with custom CSS, more boilerplate | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Static HTML + a "now" script** | open a file | ✅ | ❌ some JS | ⚠️ run a script in the terminal, then refresh |

**My view:** for "simple to run" plus "Palantir-style", **FastAPI** is the best fit, and the structure stays flat (one server file, one page). **Dash** is the fallback if you'd rather stay entirely in Python and accept a somewhat less polished look. I'd rule out Streamlit for this visual style.

How would you like to go?

==== CLAUDE (2026-10-12 12:16:03 UTC) ====
Settled: **FastAPI with a single HTML/JS page.** Below is the MVP plan. I haven't changed anything in the repo.

## Data
- **Source:** the volunteer CSV only, at oblast level. 24 regions, with Kyiv City as its own region; Luhansk and Crimea aren't in the data.
- **Training window:** from **2025‑10‑01**. Alert counts have been climbing (about 2,800 in Jan 2026 vs 4,400 in Sep), so data from 2022–24 would describe a different situation.
- **Test period:** the **last 28 days**, up to the newest record when the code runs. Everything before that is training data. Splits are by time only.
- **Rows with guessed end times** (`naive`, about 5%) are kept for model 1 and dropped for model 2.

## Model 1: P(an alert starts in the next hour)
- **One row per oblast per hour.** The label is "an alert starts in this oblast within the next hour".
- **A few basic features only:**
  - hour of day (Kyiv time) and day of week
  - which oblast
  - whether an alert is active right now
  - minutes since the last alert ended
  - number of alerts in the last 24 h and 7 d
  - how many oblasts are on alert right now, which captures nationwide waves
- **Model:** LightGBM, trained in under a minute.
- **Baseline:** the historical rate for each oblast and hour of day.
- **Metrics:** Brier score, ROC‑AUC, PR‑AUC and a calibration chart, each compared with the baseline.

## Model 2: how long an alert will last, as a distribution
- **Model:** a **log‑normal survival model** (lifelines). It outputs a full curve S(t) = P(the alert is still on after t minutes). We report the **median** as the "most likely" duration and an 80% range.
- **Inputs:** oblast, hour, how many oblasts were on alert when it started, and the length of the previous alert.
- **Conditioning on elapsed time:** in "Now" mode, if an alert has already run 20 minutes, the curve is adjusted for that. This comes for free from the survival model.
- **Baseline:** the median duration per oblast from the training data.
- **Metrics:** MAE of the median, concordance index, and how often the real duration falls inside the 80% range (it should be about 80%).

## Leakage check
A **pytest** that computes the features at time t, deletes all data after t, recomputes them, and asserts they're identical. It's cheap, and it gives the judges concrete evidence instead of a claim.

## Repo structure
```
pyproject.toml            uv; deps: pandas, lightgbm, lifelines, fastapi, uvicorn
training/                 data.py · features.py · start_model.py · duration_model.py · train.py
models/                   trained models + metrics.json + replay predictions (small, committed)
app/                      server.py · __main__.py · static/{index.html, app.js, style.css, oblasts.geojson}
tests/                    test_no_leakage.py
exploration/
```
Judges run: `uv sync` → `uv run python -m training.train` (optional) → `uv run python -m app`.

## Dashboard: a simplified dark version of your reference
- **Top bar:** title, Kyiv clock, a REPLAY / NOW switch, and a time slider over the test period.
- **Center:** map of the oblasts colored by P(start in the next hour). Oblasts where an alert was actually active are outlined in pulsing red.
- **Left:** oblasts ranked by risk. Clicking one selects it.
- **Right:** details for the selected oblast:
  - its P(start in the next hour)
  - if an alert is active: the duration curve with the median and 80% range
  - a 7‑day alert history
- **Bottom:** score tiles (model vs baseline), the calibration chart, and a nationwide hourly alert count chart.

## Open items
- **Map outline:** I still need an oblast GeoJSON file whose names match the dataset. I'll find and verify one first.
- **Timing:** about 3–4 hours for an MVP, which leaves a buffer before 23:59 for the log export, the reflection and a README.

Send your visual-style details and any changes. Once you approve, I'll start with the data and Model 1 and show you results before moving on to the UI.

    """Loading the volunteer (eTryvoga) alert dataset at oblast level.
    
    All timestamps are UTC. An alert with ``finished_at = NaT`` is treated as still active.
    """
    from pathlib import Path
    from urllib.request import urlretrieve
    
    import pandas as pd
    
    ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
    RAW_DIR = ROOT / "data" / "raw"
    MODELS_DIR = ROOT / "models"
    VOLUNTEER_URL = (
        "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/"
        "main/datasets/volunteer_data_en.csv"
    )
    VOLUNTEER_CSV = RAW_DIR / "volunteer_data_en.csv"
    
    # Alert intensity has grown steadily; older periods behave differently (see CLAUDE.md).
    TRAIN_START = pd.Timestamp("2025-10-01", tz="UTC")
    TEST_DAYS = 28
    KYIV_TZ = "Europe/Kyiv"
    
    
    def download(force: bool = False) -> Path:
        RAW_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        if force or not VOLUNTEER_CSV.exists():
            urlretrieve(VOLUNTEER_URL, VOLUNTEER_CSV)
        return VOLUNTEER_CSV
    
    
    def load_alerts(path: Path | None = None) -> pd.DataFrame:
        """Return alerts sorted by (region, started_at) with a ``duration_min`` column."""
        df = pd.read_csv(path or download(), parse_dates=["started_at", "finished_at"])
        df = df.drop_duplicates(["region", "started_at"]).sort_values(["region", "started_at"])
        df["duration_min"] = (df.finished_at - df.started_at).dt.total_seconds() / 60
        return df.reset_index(drop=True)
    
    
    def regions(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> list[str]:
        return sorted(alerts.region.unique())
    
    
    def truncate_at(alerts: pd.DataFrame, t: pd.Timestamp) -> pd.DataFrame:
        """What was knowable at time ``t``: no alert starting at/after t, unknown end for ongoing ones."""
        known = alerts[alerts.started_at < t].copy()
        ongoing = known.finished_at > t
        known.loc[ongoing, "finished_at"] = pd.NaT
        known.loc[ongoing, "duration_min"] = float("nan")
        return known
[tool output]
    File created successfully at: /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/training/data.py (file state is current in your context — no need to Read it back)
[tool call: Write]
    /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/training/features.py
    """Leakage-safe features. Every feature at time ``t`` uses only alerts that started before ``t``
    and end times that were already in the past at ``t`` (an ongoing alert is known to be active,
    but not when it will end)."""
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    
    from training.data import KYIV_TZ
    
    HOUR = np.int64(3600 * 10**9)
    MAX_GAP_MIN = 7 * 24 * 60  # cap for "minutes since last alert ended"
    
    START_FEATURES = [
        "region", "hour_kyiv", "dow_kyiv", "active_now", "min_since_last_end",
        "starts_24h", "starts_7d", "n_active_oblasts", "national_starts_1h",
    ]
    DURATION_FEATURES = ["region", "hour_sin", "hour_cos", "n_active_oblasts", "prev_duration_min"]
    
    
    def _ns(ts) -> np.ndarray:
        return pd.DatetimeIndex(ts).as_unit("ns").asi8
    
    
    def _intervals(alerts: pd.DataFrame, region: str) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        a = alerts[alerts.region == region].sort_values("started_at")
        starts = _ns(a.started_at)
        ends = np.where(a.finished_at.isna(), np.iinfo(np.int64).max, _ns(a.finished_at.fillna(a.started_at)))
        return starts, ends
    
    
    def _active_at(starts, ends, t) -> np.ndarray:
        i = np.searchsorted(starts, t, side="left") - 1  # last alert that started strictly before t
        return (i >= 0) & (ends[np.maximum(i, 0)] > t)
    
    
    def _count_between(starts, lo, hi) -> np.ndarray:
        return np.searchsorted(starts, hi, side="left") - np.searchsorted(starts, lo, side="left")
    
    
    def hourly_grid(start: pd.Timestamp, end: pd.Timestamp) -> pd.DatetimeIndex:
        return pd.date_range(start.ceil("h"), end.floor("h"), freq="h", tz="UTC")
    
    
    def start_features(alerts: pd.DataFrame, times: pd.DatetimeIndex, regions: list[str]) -> pd.DataFrame:
        """One row per (region, t) with the features known at t."""
        t = _ns(times)
        per_region = {r: _intervals(alerts, r) for r in regions}
        n_active = sum(_active_at(s, e, t).astype(int) for s, e in per_region.values())
        all_starts = np.sort(_ns(alerts.started_at))
        national_1h = _count_between(all_starts, t - HOUR, t)
        local = times.tz_convert(KYIV_TZ)
    
        frames = []
        for r, (s, e) in per_region.items():
            # ends of alerts that are already over at t (ongoing ones have end = int64 max)
            finished_ends = np.sort(e[e != np.iinfo(np.int64).max])
            j = np.searchsorted(finished_ends, t, side="right") - 1
            gap = np.where(j >= 0, (t - finished_ends[np.maximum(j, 0)]) / 6e10, MAX_GAP_MIN)
            frames.append(pd.DataFrame({
                "t": times,
                "region": r,
                "hour_kyiv": local.hour,
                "dow_kyiv": local.dayofweek,
                "active_now": _active_at(s, e, t).astype(int),
                "min_since_last_end": np.minimum(gap, MAX_GAP_MIN),
                "starts_24h": _count_between(s, t - 24 * HOUR, t),
                "starts_7d": _count_between(s, t - 7 * 24 * HOUR, t),
                "n_active_oblasts": n_active,
                "national_starts_1h": national_1h,
            }))
        out = pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True)
        out["region"] = pd.Categorical(out.region, categories=regions)
        return out
    
    
    def start_labels(alerts: pd.DataFrame, feats: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
        """1 if an alert starts in [t, t + 1h) in that region."""
        y = np.zeros(len(feats), dtype=int)
        for r, idx in feats.groupby("region", observed=True).indices.items():
            s, _ = _intervals(alerts, r)
            t = _ns(feats.t.iloc[idx])
            y[idx] = (_count_between(s, t, t + HOUR) > 0).astype(int)
        return y
    
    
    def duration_features(alerts: pd.DataFrame, events: pd.DataFrame, regions: list[str]) -> pd.DataFrame:
        """Features for alerts in ``events`` (columns region, started_at), known at their start time."""
        t = _ns(events.started_at)
        per_region = {r: _intervals(alerts, r) for r in regions}
        n_active = sum(_active_at(s, e, t).astype(int) for s, e in per_region.values())
    
        prev = np.full(len(events), np.nan)
        for r, idx in events.groupby("region").indices.items():
            a = alerts[alerts.region == r].sort_values("started_at")
            s, dur = _ns(a.started_at), a.duration_min.to_numpy()
            i = np.searchsorted(s, t[idx], side="left") - 1  # previous alert, started strictly before
            ok = i >= 0
            prev[idx[ok]] = dur[i[ok]]
    
        hour = events.started_at.dt.tz_convert(KYIV_TZ).dt.hour.to_numpy()
        out = pd.DataFrame({
            "region": events.region.to_numpy(),
            "hour_sin": np.sin(2 * np.pi * hour / 24),
            "hour_cos": np.cos(2 * np.pi * hour / 24),
            "n_active_oblasts": n_active,
            # median-ish fill when there's no previous (or it's still unknown)
            "prev_duration_min": np.nan_to_num(prev, nan=20.0),
        }, index=events.index)
        return out
[tool output]
    File created successfully at: /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/training/features.py (file state is current in your context — no need to Read it back)
[tool call: Write]
    /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/training/duration_model.py
    """Model 2: distribution of alert duration (log-normal accelerated failure time model).
    
    log(duration) ~ Normal(mu(x), sigma), so S(t) = 1 - Phi((ln t - mu) / sigma).
    The fitted coefficients are exported to JSON; prediction below needs only numpy/scipy.
    """
    import json
    from pathlib import Path
    
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from lifelines import LogNormalAFTFitter
    from lifelines.utils import concordance_index
    from scipy.stats import norm
    
    NUMERIC = ["hour_sin", "hour_cos", "n_active_oblasts", "log_prev_duration"]
    
    
    def design_matrix(feats: pd.DataFrame, regions: list[str]) -> pd.DataFrame:
        X = pd.DataFrame(index=feats.index)
        for r in regions[1:]:  # first region is the reference level
            X[f"region={r}"] = (feats.region == r).astype(float)
        X["hour_sin"], X["hour_cos"] = feats.hour_sin, feats.hour_cos
        X["n_active_oblasts"] = feats.n_active_oblasts.astype(float)
        X["log_prev_duration"] = np.log1p(feats.prev_duration_min)
        return X
    
    
    def fit(feats: pd.DataFrame, durations: pd.Series, regions: list[str]) -> tuple[dict, LogNormalAFTFitter]:
        X = design_matrix(feats, regions)
        df = X.assign(duration=durations.clip(lower=0.5).to_numpy())
        aft = LogNormalAFTFitter(penalizer=0.01).fit(df, duration_col="duration")
        mu = aft.params_.loc["mu_"]
        params = {
            "regions": regions,
            "mu_intercept": float(mu["Intercept"]),
            "mu_coef": {c: float(mu[c]) for c in X.columns},
            "log_sigma": float(aft.params_.loc[("sigma_", "Intercept")]),
        }
        return params, aft
    
    
    def predict_mu_sigma(params: dict, feats: pd.DataFrame) -> tuple[np.ndarray, float]:
        X = design_matrix(feats, params["regions"])
        coef = pd.Series(params["mu_coef"])
        mu = params["mu_intercept"] + X[coef.index].to_numpy() @ coef.to_numpy()
        return mu, float(np.exp(params["log_sigma"]))
    
    
    def quantile(mu, sigma, q, elapsed=0.0):
        """Duration quantile, optionally conditional on the alert having lasted ``elapsed`` minutes."""
        s_e = norm.sf((np.log(np.maximum(elapsed, 1e-9)) - mu) / sigma) if np.any(elapsed) else 1.0
        return np.exp(mu + sigma * norm.ppf(1 - (1 - q) * s_e))
    
    
    def survival_curve(mu: float, sigma: float, grid_min: np.ndarray, elapsed: float = 0.0) -> np.ndarray:
        s = norm.sf((np.log(grid_min) - mu) / sigma)
        if elapsed > 0:
            s = np.minimum(s / norm.sf((np.log(elapsed) - mu) / sigma), 1.0)
            s[grid_min < elapsed] = 1.0
        return s
    
    
    def evaluate(params: dict, feats: pd.DataFrame, durations: pd.Series, baseline_median: pd.Series) -> dict:
        mu, sigma = predict_mu_sigma(params, feats)
        med = np.exp(mu)
        lo, hi = quantile(mu, sigma, 0.1), quantile(mu, sigma, 0.9)
        y = durations.to_numpy()
        base = feats.region.map(baseline_median).to_numpy()
        return {
            "dur_mae_median_min": float(np.mean(np.abs(med - y))),
            "dur_mae_baseline_min": float(np.mean(np.abs(base - y))),
            "dur_medae_min": float(np.median(np.abs(med - y))),
            "dur_coverage_80": float(np.mean((y >= lo) & (y <= hi))),
            "dur_c_index": float(concordance_index(y, med)),
            "dur_mean_log_lik": float(np.mean(norm.logpdf(np.log(np.maximum(y, 0.5)), mu, sigma))),
            "sigma": sigma,
        }
    
    
    def save(params: dict, path: Path) -> None:
        path.write_text(json.dumps(params, indent=1, ensure_ascii=False))
    
    
    def load(path: Path) -> dict:
        return json.loads(path.read_text())

    """Model 1: probability that an alert starts in a region within the next hour (LightGBM)."""
    from pathlib import Path
    
    import lightgbm as lgb
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.metrics import average_precision_score, brier_score_loss, log_loss, roc_auc_score
    
    from training.features import START_FEATURES
    
    PARAMS = {
        "objective": "binary",
        "learning_rate": 0.05,
        "num_leaves": 31,
        "min_data_in_leaf": 100,
        "feature_fraction": 0.9,
        "verbose": -1,
        "seed": 0,
    }
    NUM_ROUNDS = 400
    
    
    def fit(X: pd.DataFrame, y: np.ndarray) -> lgb.Booster:
        return lgb.train(PARAMS, lgb.Dataset(X[START_FEATURES], y, categorical_feature=["region"]), NUM_ROUNDS)
    
    
    def predict(model: lgb.Booster, X: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
        return model.predict(X[START_FEATURES])
    
    
    def baseline_table(X: pd.DataFrame, y: np.ndarray) -> pd.Series:
        """Historical rate per (region, Kyiv hour) on the training set."""
        return pd.Series(y, index=X.index).groupby([X.region, X.hour_kyiv], observed=True).mean()
    
    
    def baseline_predict(table: pd.Series, X: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:
        idx = pd.MultiIndex.from_arrays([X.region.astype(str), X.hour_kyiv])
        table = table.copy()
        table.index = table.index.set_levels(table.index.levels[0].astype(str), level=0)
        return table.reindex(idx).fillna(table.mean()).to_numpy()
    
    
    def metrics(y: np.ndarray, p: np.ndarray, prefix: str) -> dict:
        p = np.clip(p, 1e-6, 1 - 1e-6)
        return {
            f"{prefix}_brier": brier_score_loss(y, p),
            f"{prefix}_logloss": log_loss(y, p),
            f"{prefix}_roc_auc": roc_auc_score(y, p),
            f"{prefix}_pr_auc": average_precision_score(y, p),
        }
    
    
    def calibration(y: np.ndarray, p: np.ndarray, bins: int = 10) -> pd.DataFrame:
        b = np.minimum((p * bins).astype(int), bins - 1)
        df = pd.DataFrame({"bin": b, "p": p, "y": y}).groupby("bin").agg(mean_pred=("p", "mean"), frac_pos=("y", "mean"), n=("y", "size"))
        return df.reset_index()
    
    
    def save(model: lgb.Booster, path: Path) -> None:
        model.save_model(str(path))
    
    
    def load(path: Path) -> lgb.Booster:
        return lgb.Booster(model_file=str(path))

    """Train both models on a time-ordered split, evaluate on the last TEST_DAYS days, save artifacts.
    
    Usage: uv run python -m training.train [--refresh] [--no-wandb]
    """
    import argparse
    import json
    import os
    import time
    
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from dotenv import load_dotenv
    
    from training import duration_model, start_model
    from training.data import MODELS_DIR, ROOT, TEST_DAYS, TRAIN_START, download, load_alerts, regions
    from training.features import START_FEATURES, duration_features, hourly_grid, start_features, start_labels
    
    
    def split_times(alerts: pd.DataFrame) -> tuple[pd.Timestamp, pd.Timestamp]:
        # Last hour whose 1h label window is fully covered by the data snapshot.
        grid_end = alerts.finished_at.max().floor("h") - pd.Timedelta(hours=1)
        return grid_end - pd.Timedelta(days=TEST_DAYS), grid_end
    
    
    def init_wandb(enabled: bool, config: dict):
        load_dotenv(ROOT / ".env")
        if not enabled or not os.getenv("WANDB_API_KEY"):
            print("W&B logging disabled")
            return None
        import wandb
        return wandb.init(project="air-raid-alerts", config=config, job_type="train")
    
    
    def main() -> None:
        ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
        ap.add_argument("--refresh", action="store_true", help="re-download the dataset")
        ap.add_argument("--no-wandb", action="store_true")
        args = ap.parse_args()
    
        download(force=args.refresh)
        alerts = load_alerts()
        regs = regions(alerts)
        test_start, grid_end = split_times(alerts)
        print(f"data until {alerts.finished_at.max()} | train {TRAIN_START} -> {test_start} | test -> {grid_end}")
        MODELS_DIR.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    
        run = init_wandb(not args.no_wandb, {
            "train_start": str(TRAIN_START), "test_start": str(test_start), "test_end": str(grid_end),
            "test_days": TEST_DAYS, "n_regions": len(regs), "start_model": start_model.PARAMS,
            "start_rounds": start_model.NUM_ROUNDS, "start_features": START_FEATURES,
        })
    
        # ---- Model 1: alert start within the next hour ----
        t0 = time.time()
        feats = start_features(alerts, hourly_grid(TRAIN_START, grid_end), regs)
        y = start_labels(alerts, feats)
        train, test = (feats.t < test_start).to_numpy(), (feats.t >= test_start).to_numpy()
        model = start_model.fit(feats[train], y[train])
        p = start_model.predict(model, feats[test])
        table = start_model.baseline_table(feats[train], y[train])
        p_base = start_model.baseline_predict(table, feats[test])
        m1 = {**start_model.metrics(y[test], p, "start"), **start_model.metrics(y[test], p_base, "start_baseline"),
              "start_train_rows": int(train.sum()), "start_test_rows": int(test.sum()),
              "start_test_pos_rate": float(y[test].mean()), "start_fit_seconds": time.time() - t0}
        calib = start_model.calibration(y[test], p)
        start_model.save(model, MODELS_DIR / "start_model.txt")
    
        # ---- Model 2: alert duration distribution ----
        t0 = time.time()
        clean = alerts[~alerts.naive & alerts.finished_at.notna()]
        tr_ev = clean[(clean.started_at >= TRAIN_START) & (clean.finished_at < test_start)]
        te_ev = clean[(clean.started_at >= test_start) & (clean.started_at <= grid_end)]
        params, aft = duration_model.fit(duration_features(alerts, tr_ev, regs), tr_ev.duration_min, regs)
        te_feats = duration_features(alerts, te_ev, regs)
        baseline_median = tr_ev.groupby("region").duration_min.median()
        m2 = duration_model.evaluate(params, te_feats, te_ev.duration_min, baseline_median)
        # sanity check: our closed-form median must match lifelines' own prediction
        X_te = duration_model.design_matrix(te_feats, regs)
        mu, sigma = duration_model.predict_mu_sigma(params, te_feats)
        gap = np.max(np.abs(np.exp(mu) - aft.predict_median(X_te).to_numpy()))
        assert gap < 1e-6 * np.exp(mu).max(), f"median mismatch vs lifelines: {gap}"
        m2.update({"dur_train_events": len(tr_ev), "dur_test_events": len(te_ev), "dur_fit_seconds": time.time() - t0})
        duration_model.save(params, MODELS_DIR / "duration_model.json")
    
        # ---- Artifacts for the dashboard replay ----
        replay = feats[test][["t", "region", "active_now", "n_active_oblasts"]].assign(
            p_start=p, p_baseline=p_base, y=y[test])
        replay["region"] = replay.region.astype(str)
        replay.to_parquet(MODELS_DIR / "replay_start.parquet", index=False)
        lookback = test_start - pd.Timedelta(days=7)  # history panel needs a week before the test window
        alerts[alerts.started_at >= lookback].to_parquet(MODELS_DIR / "replay_alerts.parquet", index=False)
        importance = dict(zip(START_FEATURES, model.feature_importance("gain").round(1).tolist()))
    
        metrics = {**m1, **m2, "test_start": str(test_start), "test_end": str(grid_end),
                   "data_until": str(alerts.finished_at.max())}
        (MODELS_DIR / "metrics.json").write_text(json.dumps(
            {"metrics": metrics, "calibration": calib.to_dict("records"), "importance": importance}, indent=1))
    
        for k, v in metrics.items():
            print(f"  {k:28s} {v:.4f}" if isinstance(v, float) else f"  {k:28s} {v}")
    
        if run:
            import wandb
            run.log({k: v for k, v in metrics.items() if isinstance(v, (int, float))})
            run.log({
                "start_calibration": wandb.plot.line(wandb.Table(dataframe=calib), "mean_pred", "frac_pos",
                                                     title="Calibration (test)"),
                "start_importance": wandb.plot.bar(wandb.Table(data=list(importance.items()), columns=["feature", "gain"]),
                                                   "feature", "gain", title="Feature importance (gain)"),
            })
            run.finish()
    
    
    if __name__ == "__main__":
        main()

    """Features at time t must be identical whether computed from the full dataset or from only
    what was knowable at t (alerts started before t, ongoing alerts with unknown end)."""
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    import pytest
    
    from training.data import load_alerts, regions, truncate_at
    from training.features import duration_features, start_features, start_labels
    
    
    @pytest.fixture(scope="module")
    def alerts():
        return load_alerts()
    
    
    def sample_times(alerts, n=40, seed=0):
        """Random hours in the last 60 days, plus exact alert start/end instants (edge cases)."""
        rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
        end = alerts.finished_at.max().floor("h")
        hours = pd.date_range(end - pd.Timedelta(days=60), end, freq="h")
        picks = list(rng.choice(hours, n, replace=False))
        recent = alerts[alerts.started_at > end - pd.Timedelta(days=60)].sample(10, random_state=seed)
        picks += list(recent.started_at) + list(recent.finished_at)
        return [pd.Timestamp(t).tz_convert("UTC") if pd.Timestamp(t).tzinfo else pd.Timestamp(t, tz="UTC") for t in picks]
    
    
    def test_start_features_use_only_past(alerts):
        regs = regions(alerts)
        for t in sample_times(alerts):
            full = start_features(alerts, pd.DatetimeIndex([t]), regs)
            past = start_features(truncate_at(alerts, t), pd.DatetimeIndex([t]), regs)
            pd.testing.assert_frame_equal(full, past, check_dtype=False, obj=f"features at {t}")
    
    
    def test_duration_features_use_only_past(alerts):
        regs = regions(alerts)
        events = alerts[alerts.started_at > alerts.started_at.max() - pd.Timedelta(days=30)].sample(50, random_state=1)
        for _, ev in events.iterrows():
            one = ev.to_frame().T.astype({"started_at": alerts.started_at.dtype})
            full = duration_features(alerts, one, regs)
            past = duration_features(truncate_at(alerts, ev.started_at), one, regs)
            pd.testing.assert_frame_equal(full, past, check_dtype=False, obj=f"duration features at {ev.started_at}")
    
    
    def test_negative_control_labels_do_depend_on_future(alerts):
        """Sanity check that the harness can detect leakage: labels look forward, so truncation changes them."""
        regs = regions(alerts)
        times = pd.DatetimeIndex(sample_times(alerts))
        feats = start_features(alerts, times, regs)
        y_full = start_labels(alerts, feats)
        y_past = np.concatenate([
            start_labels(truncate_at(alerts, t), feats[feats.t == t]) for t in times
        ])
        order = np.concatenate([np.flatnonzero((feats.t == t).to_numpy()) for t in times])
        assert y_full[order].sum() > 0 and y_past.sum() == 0

[tool call: Write]
    /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/app/prepare_geojson.py
    """One-off: turn the geoBoundaries ADM1 file into app/static/oblasts.geojson with dataset region names.
    
    Source: https://www.geoboundaries.org (gbOpen UKR ADM1, simplified), ODbL 1.0.
    Usage: uv run python -m app.prepare_geojson
    """
    import json
    from pathlib import Path
    from urllib.request import urlretrieve
    
    from app.regions import PERMANENT, REGIONS
    
    URL = ("https://github.com/wmgeolab/geoBoundaries/raw/9469f09/releaseData/gbOpen/UKR/ADM1/"
           "geoBoundaries-UKR-ADM1_simplified.geojson")
    STATIC = Path(__file__).resolve().parent / "static"
    
    
    def round_coords(c, nd=3):
        return [round(x, nd) for x in c] if isinstance(c[0], (int, float)) else [round_coords(x, nd) for x in c]
    
    
    def main() -> None:
        raw = STATIC / "ukr_adm1_raw.geojson"
        if not raw.exists():
            urlretrieve(URL, raw)
        shape_to_region = {shape: r for r, (shape, _) in REGIONS.items()}
        feats = []
        for f in json.loads(raw.read_text())["features"]:
            name = f["properties"]["shapeName"]
            region = shape_to_region.get(name) or PERMANENT[name]
            geom = {"type": f["geometry"]["type"], "coordinates": round_coords(f["geometry"]["coordinates"])}
            feats.append({"type": "Feature", "properties": {"region": region, "permanent": name in PERMANENT},
                          "geometry": geom})
        missing = set(REGIONS) - {f["properties"]["region"] for f in feats}
        assert not missing, f"regions without a shape: {missing}"
        out = STATIC / "oblasts.geojson"
        out.write_text(json.dumps({"type": "FeatureCollection", "features": feats}, separators=(",", ":")))
        raw.unlink()
        print(f"wrote {out} ({out.stat().st_size // 1024} KB, {len(feats)} features)")
    
    
    if __name__ == "__main__":
        main()

[tool call: Write]
    /home/vumenira/projects/Air_raid_alert_analysis/app/server.py
    """FastAPI backend: serves the dashboard and model outputs for replay (test period) and live "now" mode."""
    import json
    import time
    from functools import lru_cache
    from pathlib import Path
    from urllib.request import Request, urlopen
    
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
    from fastapi.responses import FileResponse
    from fastapi.staticfiles import StaticFiles
    
    from app.regions import LABELS, REGIONS, UBILLING_TO_REGION
    from training import duration_model, start_model
    from training.data import MODELS_DIR, VOLUNTEER_CSV, download, load_alerts
    from training.features import duration_features, start_features
    
    STATIC = Path(__file__).resolve().parent / "static"
    LIVE_URL = "https://ubilling.net.ua/aerialalerts/"
    CURVE_GRID = np.arange(1, 241)  # minutes, for survival curves
    
    app = FastAPI(title="Air Raid Alert Forecast")
    app.mount("/static", StaticFiles(directory=STATIC), name="static")
    
    
    @lru_cache
    def artifacts() -> dict:
        missing = [f for f in ["start_model.txt", "duration_model.json", "metrics.json", "replay_start.parquet"]
                   if not (MODELS_DIR / f).exists()]
        if missing:
            raise RuntimeError(f"missing {missing} in models/ - run `uv run python -m training.train` first")
        replay = pd.read_parquet(MODELS_DIR / "replay_start.parquet")
        alerts = pd.read_parquet(MODELS_DIR / "replay_alerts.parquet")
        dur = duration_model.load(MODELS_DIR / "duration_model.json")
        regs = dur["regions"]
        # duration distribution parameters for every alert in the replay window (features known at its start)
        alerts = alerts[alerts.region.isin(regs)].reset_index(drop=True)
        alerts["mu"], sigma = duration_model.predict_mu_sigma(dur, duration_features(alerts, alerts, regs))
        return {
            "start": start_model.load(MODELS_DIR / "start_model.txt"),
            "dur": dur, "sigma": sigma, "regions": regs,
            "metrics": json.loads((MODELS_DIR / "metrics.json").read_text()),
            "replay": replay, "alerts": alerts, "times": sorted(replay.t.unique()),
        }
    
    
    def duration_summary(mu: float, sigma: float, elapsed: float) -> dict:
        q = lambda p: float(duration_model.quantile(mu, sigma, p, elapsed))
        return {
            "elapsed_min": elapsed, "median_min": q(0.5), "p10_min": q(0.1), "p90_min": q(0.9),
            "curve": duration_model.survival_curve(mu, sigma, CURVE_GRID.astype(float), elapsed).round(4).tolist(),
        }
    
    
    @app.get("/")
    def index():
        return FileResponse(STATIC / "index.html")
    
    
    @app.get("/api/meta")
    def meta():
        a = artifacts()
        r, m = a["replay"], a["metrics"]
        national = r.groupby("t").agg(starts=("y", "sum"), active=("active_now", "sum"), p_sum=("p_start", "sum"))
        return {
            "regions": [{"region": x, "label": LABELS[x]} for x in a["regions"]],
            "times": [t.isoformat() for t in a["times"]],
            "metrics": m["metrics"], "calibration": m["calibration"], "importance": m["importance"],
            "national": {"t": [t.isoformat() for t in national.index], **{c: national[c].round(2).tolist() for c in national}},
            "curve_grid": CURVE_GRID.tolist(),
        }
    
    
    @app.get("/api/replay")
    def replay(t: str):
        a = artifacts()
        ts = pd.Timestamp(t).tz_convert("UTC") if pd.Timestamp(t).tzinfo else pd.Timestamp(t, tz="UTC")
        rows = a["replay"][a["replay"].t == ts]
        if rows.empty:
            raise HTTPException(404, f"no replay data at {ts}")
        al = a["alerts"]
        active = al[(al.started_at < ts) & ((al.finished_at > ts) | al.finished_at.isna())].set_index("region")
        out = []
        for row in rows.itertuples():
            item = {"region": row.region, "p_start": row.p_start, "p_baseline": row.p_baseline,
                    "actual_start_next_hour": int(row.y), "active": bool(row.active_now)}
            if row.region in active.index:
                al_row = active.loc[row.region]
                elapsed = (ts - al_row.started_at).total_seconds() / 60
                item["duration"] = duration_summary(al_row.mu, a["sigma"], elapsed)
                item["duration"]["started_at"] = al_row.started_at.isoformat()
                # revealed ground truth - shown for evaluation only, never used as an input
                item["duration"]["actual_min"] = None if al_row.naive else float(al_row.duration_min)
            out.append(item)
        return {"t": ts.isoformat(), "mode": "replay", "regions": out}
    
    
    @app.get("/api/region")
    def region_history(region: str, t: str):
        a = artifacts()
        ts = pd.Timestamp(t)
        r = a["replay"][a["replay"].region == region]
        al = a["alerts"]
        hist = al[(al.region == region) & (al.started_at < ts) & (al.started_at >= ts - pd.Timedelta(days=7))]
        return {
            "series": {"t": [x.isoformat() for x in r.t], "p": r.p_start.round(4).tolist(), "y": r.y.tolist()},
            "history": [{"start": s.isoformat(), "end": (min(f, ts) if pd.notna(f) else ts).isoformat()}
                        for s, f in zip(hist.started_at, hist.finished_at)],
        }
    
    
    _live_cache: dict = {}
    
    
    def live_states() -> dict[str, bool]:
        if _live_cache.get("at", 0) > time.time() - 60:
            return _live_cache["states"]
        req = Request(LIVE_URL, headers={"User-Agent": "air-raid-forecast/0.1"})
        raw = json.loads(urlopen(req, timeout=10).read())["states"]
        states = {UBILLING_TO_REGION[k]: bool(v["alertnow"]) for k, v in raw.items() if k in UBILLING_TO_REGION}
        _live_cache.update(at=time.time(), states=states)
        return states
    
    
    @app.get("/api/now")
    def now():
        a = artifacts()
        # refresh the daily dump at most once per hour
        if not VOLUNTEER_CSV.exists() or VOLUNTEER_CSV.stat().st_mtime < time.time() - 3600:
            download(force=True)
        hist = load_alerts()
        ts = pd.Timestamp.now(tz="UTC").floor("min")
        history_until = hist.finished_at.max()
        hist = hist[hist.started_at < ts]
        try:
            live = live_states()
        except Exception as e:  # live feed is optional; fall back to the dataset state
            live, live_error = None, str(e)
        else:
            live_error = None
    
        feats = start_features(hist, pd.DatetimeIndex([ts]), a["regions"])
        if live is not None:  # live state is fresher than the daily dump
            feats["active_now"] = feats.region.astype(str).map(live).fillna(feats.active_now).astype(int)
            feats["n_active_oblasts"] = int(sum(live.get(r, False) for r in a["regions"]))
        p = start_model.predict(a["start"], feats)
    
        out = []
        for (_, row), pr in zip(feats.iterrows(), p):
            item = {"region": str(row.region), "p_start": float(pr), "active": bool(row.active_now)}
            if row.active_now:
                # start time of a live alert is unknown -> unconditional distribution (elapsed = 0)
                ev = pd.DataFrame({"region": [str(row.region)], "started_at": [ts]})
                df = duration_features(hist, ev, a["regions"])
                df["n_active_oblasts"] = row.n_active_oblasts
                mu, sigma = duration_model.predict_mu_sigma(a["dur"], df)
                item["duration"] = duration_summary(float(mu[0]), sigma, 0.0)
            out.append(item)
        return {"t": ts.isoformat(), "mode": "now", "history_until": history_until.isoformat(),
                "live_source": None if live is None else LIVE_URL, "live_error": live_error, "regions": out}

    cat >> tests/test_no_leakage.py <<'EOF'
    
    
    def test_region_without_alerts(alerts):
        """A region with no history (e.g. a quiet oblast in a short window) must not crash."""
        regs = regions(alerts)
        t = alerts.started_at.max()
        subset = alerts[alerts.region == regs[0]]
        feats = start_features(subset, pd.DatetimeIndex([t]), regs)
        quiet = feats[feats.region != regs[0]]
        assert (quiet.active_now == 0).all() and (quiet.starts_7d == 0).all()
        ev = subset.tail(3)
        assert len(duration_features(subset, ev, regs)) == 3
    EOF

[tool output]
    # Reference palette
    
    This is the **reference instance** of the data-viz method: every parameter the
    method needs, filled in with a validated default palette. The rest of the skill
    is system-agnostic - **to target your brand, substitute this file's values** and
    re-run the validator. Nothing else changes.
    
    ## How to use these values
    
    Everything below is plain hex. In an HTML chart, **define the slots you use as
    CSS custom properties in a local `<style>` block** at the top of the file, then
    reference them by role throughout - so the light/dark values swap in one place,
    and the chart body is written against roles rather than raw hex:
    
    ```css
    .viz-root {
      color-scheme: light;
      --surface-1:      #fcfcfb;   /* chart surface */
      --text-primary:   #0b0b0b;
      --text-secondary: #52514e;
      --series-1:       #2a78d6;   /* categorical slot 1 */
      /* ...only the roles this chart uses */
    }
    @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {
      :root:where(:not([data-theme="light"])) .viz-root {
        color-scheme: dark;
        --surface-1:      #1a1a19;
        --text-primary:   #f0efec;
        --text-secondary: #c3c2b7;
        --series-1:       #3987e5;
      }
    }
    :root[data-theme="dark"] .viz-root {
      color-scheme: dark;
      --surface-1:      #1a1a19;
      --text-primary:   #f0efec;
      --text-secondary: #c3c2b7;
      --series-1:       #3987e5;
    }
    ```
    
    Declare the dark values under both scopes as above - the media query covers
    the OS setting; the `data-theme` scope covers the viewer's theme toggle,
    which must win both ways (the `:not(...)` guard lets a light stamp beat
    OS-dark; `:where()` keeps the media block below the toggle scope).
    
    ## Categorical palette
    
    Both modes are selected. The dark column is the same eight hues stepped for the
    dark surface, not a separate palette:
    
    | Slot | Hue | Light | Dark |
    |------|-----|-------|------|
    | 1 | blue | `#2a78d6` | `#3987e5` |
    | 2 | orange | `#eb6834` | `#d95926` |
    | 3 | aqua | `#1baf7a` | `#199e70` |
    | 4 | yellow | `#eda100` | `#c98500` |
    | 5 | magenta | `#e87ba4` | `#d55181` |
    | 6 | green | `#008300` | `#008300` |
    | 7 | violet | `#6250d6` | `#9085e9` |
    | 8 | red | `#e34948` | `#e66767` |
    
    This order passes every hard gate in both modes on the default *adjacent*
    pairlist (stacks, bars, lines): worst adjacent CVD Delta E 9.1 light / 8.4 dark
    (OKLab ×100, >=8 target), worst adjacent normal-vision Delta E 19.6 light / 19.3
    dark (>=15 floor). Under `--pairs all` (scatter, bubble, choropleth, small
    multiples) the full eight cannot clear the floors - with all 28 pairs in
    play no ordering can (the pairlist no longer depends on order), and
    re-stepping is off the table by the documented-palette rule - so those
    chart forms carry a series cap: **the first three slots validate all-pairs
    in both modes** (worst pair CVD Delta E 9.2 light / 9.4 dark, normal-vision 24.0
    light / 20.9 dark - clear of the CVD warn band). Past three, fold to "Other" or
    facet: the fourth slot puts yellow and orange on screen
    together, and that pair fails the all-pairs floors (normal-vision 13.7
    light; CVD 4.8 dark). Three light-mode slots (magenta, yellow, aqua)
    sit below 3:1 contrast on the light surface: the **relief rule** applies (ship
    visible direct labels or the table view). The dark steps were chosen for the
    dark band (OKLCH L ~ 0.48-0.67, >= 3:1 on the dark surface) and validated as a
    set. (Ordering history: adopted July 2026 for its more harmonious opening -
    the same eight hues and steps as its predecessor, re-ordered, zero hex
    changes. The predecessor validated its first FOUR slots all-pairs, with its
    dark run in the 6-8 CVD warn band, so secondary encoding was required there;
    this order deliberately trades that fourth slot - yellow now sits beside orange -
    for better-looking leading colors. Revisit the trade if yellow<->orange
    confusion shows up in real charts with four or more series; undoing it is a
    pure re-order. Light slot 7 has since been lightened, `#4a3aa7` -> `#6250d6`
    (August 2026); the adjacent-pair figures above are unchanged by that move, the
    non-adjacent blue<->violet pair now sits below both floors under `--pairs all`
    (normal-vision 10.5, CVD 4.5 - inside the range the series cap already
    excludes), and the order enumeration was run with the earlier value.) When you
    swap in your own ramps, hold your palette to the full gate.
    
    The slot **ordering** is the CVD-safety mechanism, not cosmetic - candidate
    orderings were enumerated and only those clearing every adjacent gate in both
    modes kept (see `color-formula.md` § Themes); this default is one of the
    passing orders, picked among them for its opening colors. When you swap in
    your brand's hues, do the same: run the validator on candidate orderings and
    choose only among the passing ones.
    
    ## Sequential hue
    
    Default single hue: **blue**, light->dark. When two sequential contexts appear at
    once, the second takes the next categorical slot's hue (orange), each as its own
    one-hue ramp.
    
    | step | hex | step | hex | step | hex | step | hex |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | 100 | `#cde2fb` | 250 | `#86b6ef` | 400 | `#3987e5` | 550 | `#1c5cab` |
    | 150 | `#b7d3f6` | 300 | `#6da7ec` | 450 | `#2a78d6` | 600 | `#184f95` |
    | 200 | `#9ec5f4` | 350 | `#5598e7` | 500 | `#256abf` | 650 | `#104281` |
    | | | | | | | 700 | `#0d366b` |
    
    The full 100->700 range is for **sequential** encoding (continuous magnitude -
    heatmaps, choropleths) where the lightest step means "near zero" and is allowed
    to recede toward the surface. For an **ordinal** ramp (discrete ordered marks -
    funnel stages, tiers - validated with `--ordinal`), the step nearest the surface
    must still clear 2:1: on light, start no lighter than **step 250** (`#86b6ef`,
    2.06:1); on dark, go no darker than **step 600** (`#184f95`, 2.15:1).
    
    ## Diverging pair
    
    **blue <-> red** - warm/cool poles that read as opposite. Neutral midpoint is gray
    (light `#f0efec`, dark `#383835`). Equal step count per arm. (blue<->aqua was
    rejected - both cool, the midpoint doesn't read as "nothing".)
    
    ## Status palette (fixed - never themed)
    
    | role | hex | light-surface contrast | dark-surface contrast |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | good | `#0ca30c` | 3.27 | 5.19 |
    | warning | `#fab219` | 1.79 | 9.49 |
    | serious | `#ec835a` | 2.57 | 6.60 |
    | critical | `#d03b3b` | 4.68 | 3.62 |
    
    Dark: same four steps - all clear 3:1 on the dark surface (`#1a1a19`) and remain
    distinct from the dark categorical slots. On the light surface, warning and
    serious are sub-3:1 by design; the **icon + label** pairing is the mitigation, so
    a status color never carries meaning alone. These steps are deliberately distinct
    from the categorical slots so a status color never impersonates a series -
    distinct enough that nothing collides at a glance, not enough for hue to
    carry the distinction unaided: measured by the series floor's own bar
    (unsimulated Delta E >= 15), around nine categorical-vs-status pairs per mode sit
    below 15 - in light mode red vs critical and yellow vs warning both measure
    4.8, slot-2 orange sits 5.8 from status-serious, and the light success text
    green `#006300` sits 10.1 from the series green; green vs status-good (9.7)
    holds in both modes, since both hexes are mode-invariant. The rule is general: any series color beside a
    same-hue-family status or delta cue leans on the icon + label pairing and on
    placement; never on hue alone.
    
    ## Texture fill (the accessibility channel)
    [... 1 more lines truncated]
